To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} \), I can use the Taylor series expansion for cosine.

First, recall that the Taylor series expansion for \( \cos(x) \) around 0 is:

\( \cos(x) = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2!} + \frac{x^4}{4!} - \ldots \)

Subtracting this from 1 gives:

\( 1 - \cos(x) = \frac{x^2}{2!} - \frac{x^4}{4!} + \ldots \)

Now, divide by \( x^2 \):

\( \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} = \frac{1}{2} - \frac{x^2}{24} + \ldots \)

As \( x \) approaches 0, the higher-order terms become negligible. Therefore, the limit simplifies to:

\( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} = \frac{1}{2} \)
</think>

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} \), we can use the Taylor series expansion for cosine.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Taylor Series Expansion of Cosine:**
   The Taylor series expansion of \( \cos(x) \) around 0 is:
   \[
   \cos(x) = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2!} + \frac{x^4}{4!} - \ldots
   \]

2. **Subtracting from 1:**
   Subtract the Taylor series of \( \cos(x) \) from 1:
   \[
   1 - \cos(x) = \frac{x^2}{2!} - \frac{x^4}{4!} + \ldots
   \]

3. **Dividing by \( x^2 \):**
   Divide both sides of the equation by \( x^2 \):
   \[
   \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} = \frac{1}{2} - \frac{x^2}{24} + \ldots
   \]

4. **Taking the Limit as \( x \) Approaches 0:**
   As \( x \) approaches 0, the higher-order terms (\( x^2 \), etc.) become negligible:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{1}{2}}

So, the original expression is sqrt(x² + 2x) - x. To rationalize this, I'll multiply both the numerator and denominator (even though it's technically just the same as the original since we're considering the limit) by the conjugate, which is sqrt(x² + 2x) + x.

That gives me [sqrt(x² + 2x) - x] * [sqrt(x² + 2x) + x] divided by [sqrt(x² + 2x) + x]. 

Multiplying out the numerator using the difference of squares formula, it becomes (x² + 2x) minus x². Simplifying that, the x² terms cancel out, leaving just 2x in the numerator.

So now, the expression is 2x divided by [sqrt(x² + 2x) + x]. 

Next, I can factor x from both terms inside the square root to simplify further. So sqrt(x²(1 + 2/x)) becomes x*sqrt(1 + 2/x). Therefore, the denominator becomes x*sqrt(1 + 2/x) + x.

Factoring out x from the denominator gives me x[ sqrt(1 + 2/x) + 1 ]. 

Now, substituting back into the expression, we have (2x) divided by [x(sqrt(1 + 2/x) + 1)]. The x in the numerator and denominator cancels out, leaving us with 2 / [sqrt(1 + 2/x) + 1].

As x approaches infinity, the term 2/x inside the square root approaches zero. Therefore, sqrt(1 + 0) is just 1. So the expression simplifies to 2/(1 + 1), which equals 2/2.

Hence, the limit as x approaches infinity of sqrt(x² + 2x) minus x is 1.
</think>

To find the limit \( \lim_{x \to \infty} \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \):

1. **Rationalize by Multiplying by Conjugate**:
   Multiply numerator and denominator by the conjugate to simplify.

   \[
   \frac{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x}{1} \times \frac{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
   \]

2. **Simplify the Numerator**:
   Using the difference of squares:

   \[
   (x^2 + 2x) - x^2 = 2x
   \]

3. **Rewrite Denominator**:
   Factor \( x \) from inside the square root.

   \[
   \sqrt{x^2(1 + \frac{2}{x})} + x = x\left(\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1\right)
   \]

4. **Cancel Out \( x \)**:
   Simplify expression by canceling \( x \) in numerator and denominator.

5. **Evaluate the Limit**:
   As \( x \to \infty \), \( \frac{2}{x} \to 0 \).

   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + 0} + 1} = \frac{2}{2} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer**:  
The limit is \( 1 \).

The limit can be evaluated by recognizing standard limits and applying L’Hospital’s Rule, both confirming the result.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express tan(x) in terms of sine and cosine:**
   \[
   \tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)}
   \]
   
2. **Rewrite the original limit:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan(x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x \cdot \cos(x)}
   \]
   
3. **Separate into two known limits:**
   \[
   \left( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} \right) \cdot \left( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{\cos(x)} \right)
   \]
   
4. **Evaluate each limit:**
   - \(\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1\) (Standard limit)
   - \(\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{\cos(x)} = \frac{1}{\cos(0)} = 1\) (Since cos(0) = 1)

5. **Multiply the results:**
   \[
   1 \cdot 1 = 1
   \]
   
6. **Alternative verification using L’Hospital’s Rule:**
   - Differentiate numerator and denominator:
     - \( \frac{d}{dx} \tan(x) = \sec^2(x) \)
     - \( \frac{d}{dx} x = 1 \)
   - The limit becomes:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sec^2(x)}{1}
     \]
   - Since \( \sec(0) = 1 \), the result is 1.

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

   - Expand \(\tan x\) around \(x = 0\):
     \[
     \tan x = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \dots

   - Subtract this from \(x\):
     \[
     x - \tan x = x - \left(x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \dots\right) = -\frac{x^3}{3} - \frac{2x^5}{15} + \dots
     \]
   - For small \(x\), higher-order terms can be neglected, so:
     \[
     x - \tan x \approx -\frac{x^3}{3}
     \]
   - Substitute into the original limit:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{-\frac{x^3}{3}}{x^3} = \lim_{x \to 0} -\frac{1}{3} = -\frac{1}{3}
     \]

2. **L'Hospital's Rule Approach:**
   - The original limit is in the indeterminate form \(0/0\) when substituting \(x=0\).
   - Apply L'Hospital's Rule three times:
     - First derivative:
       \[
       f'(x) = 1 - \sec^2 x, \quad g'(x) = 3x^2
       \]
     - Second derivative:
       \[
       f''(x) = -2\sec^2 x \tan x, \quad g''(x) = 6x
       \]
     - Third derivative:
       \[
       f'''(x) = -4\sec^2 x \tan^2 x - 2\sec^4 x, \quad g'''(x) = 6
       \]
   - Evaluate at \(x=0\):
     \[
     f'''(0) = -4(1)(0)^2 - 2(1) = -2, \quad g'''(0) = 6
     \]
   - Thus:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3} = \frac{f'''(0)}{g'''(0)} = \frac{-2}{6} = -\frac{1}{3}
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The limit is \(-\frac{1}{3}\).

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using the power rule. Rewriting \( \frac{1}{x} \) as \( x^{-1} \), we apply the power rule:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left(x^n\right) = n \cdot x^{n-1}
\]

Here, \( n = -1 \):

\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left(x^{-1}\right) = -1 \cdot x^{-2} = -\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

Thus, the derivative is:

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \ln x \), we use the standard rule for differentiating natural logarithms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Function:**  
   The given function is \( f(x) = \ln x \).

2. **Apply the Derivative Rule for Natural Logarithm:**  
   The derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is a well-known result in calculus:  
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}
   \]

3. **Differentiate Term by Term (if applicable):**  
   Since the function only contains one term, there's no need to differentiate each term separately.

4. **Combine the Results:**  
   Putting it all together, the derivative of \( f(x) = \ln x \) is:  
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( f(x) = \ln x \) is \( f'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

Apply the power rule to each term in \( f(x) \):

\[
f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) - \frac{d}{dx}(3x^3) + \frac{d}{dx}(x)

Calculate each derivative:

- \(\frac{d}{dx}(x^4) = 4x^3\)
- \(\frac{d}{dx}(3x^3) = 9x^2\)
- \(\frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1\)

So, the first derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative
Take the derivative of \( f'(x) \):

\[

\]

Calculate each derivative:

- \(\frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) = 12x^2\)
- \(\frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) = 18x\)
- \(\frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0\)

So, the second derivative is:

\[
f''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x

The derivative of \( \tan x \) is found using the quotient rule on \( \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \), resulting in \( \sec^2 x \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express tan(x):**
   \( f(x) = \tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \)

2. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   - Let \( g(x) = \sin x \), so \( g'(x) = \cos x \)
   - Let \( s(x) = \cos x \), so \( s'(x) = -\sin x \)
   
3. **Compute f'(x):**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)s(x) - g(x)s'(x)}{[s(x)]^2} = \frac{\cos x \cdot \cos x - \sin x \cdot (-\sin x)}{\cos^2 x}
   \]

4. **Simplify the Numerator:**
   \[
   \cos^2 x + \sin^2 x = 1
   \]
   
5. **Final Derivative:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x} = \sec^2 x
   \]

**Answer:**  
{Final Answer: "sec²(x)"}

The derivative of f(x) = cos(x) · ln(x) is found using the product rule: 

f’(x) = -sin(x)·ln(x) + (cos(x)/x).

Final Answer:
{Final Answer: "-\\sin(x) \\cdot \\ln(x) + \\frac{\\cos(x)}{x}"}

Step-by-Step Explanation:
1. **Identify the functions:** Let u(x) = cos(x) and v(x) = ln(x).
2. **Differentiate each function:**
   - u’(x) = derivative of cos(x) is -sin(x).
   - v’(x) = derivative of ln(x) is 1/x.
3. **Apply the product rule:** f’(x) = u’(x)v(x) + u(x)v’(x).
4. **Substitute the derivatives:**
   - f’(x) = (-sin(x))·ln(x) + cos(x)·(1/x).
5. **Simplify if necessary:** The expression is already simplified.

The derivative of \( y = x^x \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using logarithmic differentiation. 

1. Take the natural logarithm of both sides: \( \ln(y) = x \ln(x) \).
2. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \): \( \frac{1}{y} y' = \ln(x) + 1 \).
3. Multiply both sides by \( y \): \( y' = y (\ln(x) + 1) \).
4. Substitute \( y = x^x \): \( y' = x^x (1 + \ln(x)) \).

So, the derivative is:

\[
\boxed{x^{x}(1+\ln(x))}
\]

To solve this, I'll use the product rule from calculus. The product rule states that if you have a function which is the product of two functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative is:

\[ (u \cdot v)' = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v' \]

In this case, I can let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = e^x \)

Now, I'll find the derivatives of each part:
- The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) is \( u'(x) = 1 \).
- The derivative of \( v(x) = e^x \) is \( v'(x) = e^x \).

\[ f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \]

\[ f'(x) = (1 + x) \cdot e^x \]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we can use the **Product Rule** from calculus. The Product Rule states that if you have a function which is the product of two functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative is:

\[
(u \cdot v)' = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v'
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

Now, find the derivatives of each part:
1. The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) is \( u'(x) = 1 \).
2. The derivative of \( v(x) = e^x \) is \( v'(x) = e^x \).

Applying the Product Rule:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
f'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
\]

Simplify by factoring out \( e^x \):

\[
f'(x) = (1 + x)e^x
\]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( f(x) = x \cdot e^x \) is:

\[
\boxed{(x + 1)e^x}
\]

\[

To find the derivative of the function:

\[ f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \]

we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Rewrite the Function
First, rewrite the function using a negative exponent for easier differentiation:

\[ f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \]

### Step 2: Apply the Chain Rule
The chain rule states that if \( h(x) = g(f(x)) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is:

\[ h'(x) = g'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x) \]

In this case:
- The outer function \( g(u) = u^{-1} \)
- The inner function \( u(x) = x^2 + 1 \)

First, find the derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \):

\[ \frac{d}{du} (u^{-1}) = -u^{-2} \]

Next, find the derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \):

\[ \frac{d}{dx} (x^2 + 1) = 2x \]

### Step 3: Combine Using the Chain Rule
Multiply the derivatives from the outer and inner functions:

\[
f'(x) = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x \\
f'(x) = -(x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x \\
f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

### Final Answer
The derivative of the function is:

\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{\left(x^{2}+1\right)^{2}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), I'll use the quotient rule, which states that if you have a function \( \frac{u}{v} \), its derivative is \( \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} \).

Here, \( u = \ln x \) and \( v = x^2 \). 

First, I'll find the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \):
- The derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \).
- The derivative of \( x^2 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2x \).

Now, applying the quotient rule:
\( f'(x) = \frac{\left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2} \)

Simplifying each term in the numerator:
- \( \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) = x \)
- \( (\ln x)(2x) = 2x \ln x \)

So, the expression becomes:
\( f'(x) = \frac{x - 2x \ln x}{x^4} \)

Finally, I can factor out an \( x \) in the numerator to simplify further:
\( f'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2\ln x)}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^3} \)

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule** from calculus. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:

\[
h'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Here, let:
- \( u(x) = \ln x \)
- \( v(x) = x^2 \)

First, we find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (x^2) = 2x
\]

Now, applying the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{\left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
\]

Simplifying each term in the numerator:
\[
\left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) = x
\]
\[
(\ln x)(2x) = 2x \ln x
\]

So, the expression becomes:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{x - 2x \ln x}{x^4}
\]

Finally, we can factor out an \( x \) in the numerator to simplify further:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2\ln x)}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^3}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1 - 2\ln(x)}{x^{3}}}
\]

The function f(x) = ln(sin x) requires the application of the chain rule for differentiation.

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Outer function: ln(u)
   - Inner function: u = sin x

2. **Differentiate each part:**
   - Derivative of ln(u) with respect to u is 1/u.
   - Derivative of sin x with respect to x is cos x.

3. **Apply the chain rule:**
   Multiply the derivatives together:
   (1/u) * cos x = (1/sin x) * cos x

4. **Simplify if possible:**
   This can be written as cot x or simplified further, but both forms are correct.

**Final Answer:** The derivative of f(x) is (cos x)/(sin x), which can also be expressed as cot x.

The derivative of the function \( f(x) = x \ln x \) can be found using the product rule. 

Let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

Then:
- \( u'(x) = 1 \)
- \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

Next up is h'(x), which is the derivative of (x² + 1) with respect to x. Breaking it down, the derivative of x squared is 2x, and the derivative of 1 is zero because constants vanish when you take their derivatives. So h'(x) should be 2x.

Now I can plug these into the quotient rule formula. The numerator becomes g'(x)h(x) minus g(x)h'(x). Substituting the known values, that's cos x times (x² + 1) minus sin x times 2x. So far so good.

The limit can be found by simplifying the expression.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. The original expression is \(\frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}\).

2. Factor the numerator using the difference of cubes formula:
   \(x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)\).

3. Substitute the factored form into the expression:
   \(\frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}\).

4. Cancel out the common factor \(x - 1\) (valid for \(x \neq 1\)):
   \(x^2 + x + 1\).

5. Take the limit as \(x\) approaches 1:
   \(\lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3\).

Answer: {"Final Answer": "3"}

Let me recall what L'Hospital's Rule says: if we have a limit of the form f(x)/g(x) where both f and g approach either 0 or infinity as x approaches some value (could be finite or infinite), and if the derivatives f'(x) and g'(x) exist, then the limit can be found by taking the ratio of their derivatives. So in this case, since both e^x and x² go to infinity, L'Hospital's Rule applies.

So applying it once: take the derivative of the numerator, which is d/dx (e^x) = e^x, and the derivative of the denominator, d/dx (x²) = 2x. So now we have lim(x→∞) [e^x / (2x)]. 

But wait, as x approaches infinity, e^x still goes to infinity and 2x also goes to infinity, so this new limit is again of the form ∞/∞. That means I can apply L'Hospital's Rule a second time.

Taking derivatives again: derivative of e^x remains e^x, and derivative of 2x is 2. So now we have lim(x→∞) [e^x / 2]. 

This limit is straightforward because the numerator grows exponentially while the denominator is just a constant. As x approaches infinity, e^x becomes infinitely large, so dividing it by 2 doesn't change the fact that it's still infinity.

Therefore, putting it all together: after applying L'Hospital's Rule twice, we find that the limit is infinity.
</think>

To find the limit of the given expression as \( x \) approaches infinity:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{5x^3 + 2x}{7x^3 + 3x^2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Highest Power of \( x \):**
   
   Both the numerator and denominator have terms with the highest power of \( x \) being \( x^3 \).

2. **Divide Each Term by the Highest Power (\( x^3 \)):**
   
   To simplify, divide each term in both the numerator and the denominator by \( x^3 \):
   
   \[
   \frac{5x^3 + 2x}{7x^3 + 3x^2} = \frac{\frac{5x^3}{x^3} + \frac{2x}{x^3}}{\frac{7x^3}{x^3} + \frac{3x^2}{x^3}}
   \]
   
   Simplifying further:
   
   \[
   = \frac{5 + \frac{2}{x^2}}{7 + \frac{3}{x}}
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit as \( x \to \infty \):**
   
   As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms with \( \frac{1}{x} \) and higher powers approach zero:
   
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{5 + 0}{7 + 0} = \frac{5}{7}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{5}{7}}
\]

The limit of \(\frac{\tan x}{x}\) as \(x\) approaches 0 can be found by recognizing that \(\tan x = \sin x / \cos x\). Breaking it down, we have:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan x}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \left( \frac{\sin x}{x} \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \right)
\]

We know that \(\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1\) and \(\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{\cos x} = 1\). Therefore, multiplying these limits gives:

\[
1 \cdot 1 = 1
\]

So in this case, my f(x) is ln(1+x) and g(x) is x. Let me find their derivatives.

The derivative of ln(1+x) with respect to x is 1/(1+x), right? And the derivative of x with respect to x is just 1. So applying L'Hospital's Rule, the limit becomes:

lim_{x→0} [ (1/(1+x)) / 1 ]

Which simplifies to lim_{x→0} 1/(1+x). Now plugging in x=0 into this expression gives me 1/(1+0) = 1. So that should be the limit.

Wait, but maybe I can also think of another way without using L'Hospital's Rule. Sometimes Taylor series expansion helps with limits as well. Let me recall the expansion for ln(1+x). The natural logarithm of (1+x) around x=0 is approximately x - x²/2 + x³/3 - ... So if I expand it, it would be:

ln(1+x) ≈ x - (x²)/2 + higher order terms.

So when x is near 0, the higher order terms become negligible. Therefore, ln(1+x) is approximately equal to x for small x. Thus, dividing both numerator and denominator by x gives me [x]/[x] = 1, so as x approaches 0, this ratio should approach 1.

That confirms what I got using L'Hospital's Rule. So it seems like the limit is indeed 1.

I wonder if there's another method to verify this. Maybe using the definition of derivative? Because sometimes derivatives can be used to evaluate limits that represent them.

Wait, let me think about this: The derivative of a function at a point is the limit as h approaches 0 of [f(a+h) - f(a)] / h. In particular, if I take f(x) = ln(1+x), then its derivative at x=0 would be:

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} \) is calculated by recognizing it represents the derivative of \( \ln(1+x) \) at \( x=0 \), which evaluates to 1.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Form:** As \( x \to 0 \), both numerator \( \ln(1 + x) \) and denominator \( x \) approach 0, resulting in a 0/0 indeterminate form.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:** Differentiate numerator and denominator:
   - Derivative of numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(1+x)] = \frac{1}{1+x} \)
   - Derivative of denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1 \)

3. **Simplify the New Expression:** The limit becomes \( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{1+x} \).

4. **Evaluate at \( x=0 \):** Substituting \( x=0 \) gives \( \frac{1}{1+0} = 1 \).

5. **Alternative Verification Using Taylor Series:** Expand \( \ln(1+x) \approx x - \frac{x^2}{2} + \cdots \). Dividing by \( x \) yields approximately 1 as higher-order terms vanish.

6. **Derivative Interpretation:** Recognize the limit as the definition of the derivative of \( f(x) = \ln(1+x) \) at \( x=0 \), which is \( f'(0) = \frac{1}{1+0} = 1 \).

**Final Answer:**

The limit is **1**.

   - Differentiate using the chain rule:

To find the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \), we can use basic differentiation rules.

### Step 1: Rewrite the Function
First, rewrite \( \frac{1}{x} \) using a negative exponent:
\[
\frac{1}{x} = x^{-1}
\]

### Step 2: Apply the Power Rule
The power rule states that for any function \( f(x) = x^n \), where \( n \) is any real number, the derivative is:
\[
f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1}
\]
Applying this to our function:
\[
\frac{d}{dx} (x^{-1}) = -1 \cdot x^{-1-1} = -x^{-2}
\]

### Step 3: Simplify the Expression
Simplify \( -x^{-2} \) back to a fraction form:
\[
-x^{-2} = -\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

### Final Answer
The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{1}{x^{2}}}
\]

\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u(x)v(x)] = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using logarithmic differentiation. 

1. Let \( y = x^x \).
2. Take the natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = x \ln(x) \).
3. Differentiate both sides:
   - Left side: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \)
   - Right side: Apply product rule to \( x \ln(x) \), resulting in \( \ln(x) + 1 \).
4. Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \): Multiply both sides by \( y \) (which is \( x^x \)).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we will use the **Product Rule**. The Product Rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let's apply this rule to our function:

1. **Identify the two functions**:
   - Let \( u(x) = x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

2. **Find the derivatives of each function**:
   - \( u'(x) = 1 \) (since the derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1)
   - \( v'(x) = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^x \))

3. **Apply the Product Rule**:
   \[
   f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives we found:
   \[
   f'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression**:
   Factor out \( e^x \):
   \[
   f'(x) = e^x (1 + x)
   \]
   
So, the derivative of \( f(x) = x \cdot e^x \) is:

\[
f'(x) = e^x (1 + x)
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{e^{x} (x + 1)}
\]

To find the derivative of \(\sin(x) \cdot \ln(x)\), we use the product rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the functions:** Let \( u = \sin(x) \) and \( v = \ln(x) \).

2. **Differentiate each function:**
   - The derivative of \( u = \sin(x) \) is \( u' = \cos(x) \).
   - The derivative of \( v = \ln(x) \) is \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \).

3. **Apply the product rule:** 
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}[u \cdot v] = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v'
   \]
   Substituting in:
   \[
   \cos(x) \cdot \ln(x) + \sin(x) \cdot \frac{1}{x}
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:** 
   The resulting derivative is:
   \[
   \cos(x)\ln(x) + \frac{\sin(x)}{x}
   \]

To find the second derivative of \( f(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll follow these steps:

1. **First Derivative:**
   Apply the product rule to \( f(x) = u \cdot v \), where:
   - \( u = x^2 \)
   - \( v = e^x \)

   Compute the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \):
   - \( u' = 2x \)
   - \( v' = e^x \)

   Apply the product rule:
   \[
   f'(x) = u'v + uv' = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
   \]
   Factor out \( e^x \):
   \[
   f'(x) = e^x (x^2 + 2x)
   \]

2. **Second Derivative:**
   Differentiate \( f'(x) \) again using the product rule:
   - Let’s consider \( f'(x) = u_1 \cdot v_1 \), where:
     - \( u_1 = x^2 + 2x \)
     - \( v_1 = e^x \)

   Compute the derivatives of \( u_1 \) and \( v_1 \):
   - \( u'_1 = 2x + 2 \)
   - \( v'_1 = e^x \)

   Apply the product rule:
   \[
   f''(x) = u'_1 v_1 + u_1 v'_1 = (2x + 2)e^x + (x^2 + 2x)e^x
   \]
   Factor out \( e^x \):
   \[
   f''(x) = e^x (2x + 2 + x^2 + 2x)
   \]
   Combine like terms:
   \[
   f''(x) = e^x (x^2 + 4x + 2)
   \]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we can use the chain rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Rewrite the Function:**
   
   First, rewrite \( f(x) \) as:
   \[
   f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   
   The chain rule states that if \( h(x) = g(f(x)) \), then \( h'(x) = g'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x) \).
   
   Here, let:
   - Outer function: \( g(u) = u^{-1} \)
   - Inner function: \( u = x^2 + 1 \)

3. **Differentiate the Outer Function:**
   
   Differentiate \( g(u) \) with respect to \( u \):
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} [u^{-1}] = -u^{-2}
   \]

4. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**
   
   Differentiate \( u = x^2 + 1 \) with respect to \( x \):
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} [x^2 + 1] = 2x
   \]

5. **Combine Using the Chain Rule:**
   
   Multiply the derivatives from steps 3 and 4:
   \[
   f'(x) = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x
   \]
   
   Substitute \( u = x^2 + 1 \):
   \[
   f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the **chain rule**. 

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions**:
   - Outer function: \( \ln(u) \)
   - Inner function: \( u = \sin x \)

2. **Differentiate each part**:
   - Derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \): \( \frac{d}{du}[\ln(u)] = \frac{1}{u} \)
   - Derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \): \( \frac{d}{dx}[\sin x] = \cos x \)

3. **Apply the chain rule**:
\[

4. **Simplify the expression**:
\[

\]

The derivative of \( f(x) = \ln(\sin x) \) is \boxed{\cot x}.

To find the second derivative of \( f(x) = x^3 \ln x \), we proceed as follows:

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), then its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

Let:
\[
u(x) = x \\
v(x) = 1 + x^2
\]

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \\
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(1 + x^2) = 2x
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

Substitute \( u(x) \), \( v(x) \), \( u'(x) \), and \( v'(x) \) into the quotient rule formula:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} \\
= \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Numerator**

\[
(1)(1 + x^2) = 1 + x^2 \\
(x)(2x) = 2x^2
\]

So, the numerator becomes:
\[
1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

**Step 5: Write the Final Derivative**

\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1 - x^{2}}{(1 + x^{2})^{2}}}
\]

A2 ≤ A1 ≤ A3

(1/2)*sin(x) ≤ (1/2)*x ≤ (1/2)*tan(x)

sin(x) ≤ x ≤ tan(x)

sin(x) ≤ x ≤ sin(x)/cos(x)

1 ≤ x/sin(x) ≤ 1/cos(x)

Taking the reciprocal of each part reverses the inequalities:

1 ≥ sin(x)/x ≥ cos(x)

Now, as x approaches 0 from the right (i.e., x→0+), what happens to cos(x)? It approaches 1. So we have:

lim_{x→0+} sin(x)/x is squeezed between 1 and something approaching 1.

Hence, by the squeeze theorem, lim_{x→0+} sin(x)/x = 1.

lim_{x→0} [d/dx sin(x)] / [d/dx x] = lim_{x→0} cos(x)/1

As x approaches 0, cos(0) equals 1. So again, the limit is 1.

This confirms our earlier result using a different method. Therefore, I'm pretty confident that the limit of sin(x)/x as x approaches 0 is indeed 1.

The limit of \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side is infinity.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Problem:** We are asked to find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 from the right (positive values of \( x \)) for the function \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2} \).

2. **Analyze Behavior Near Zero:** When \( x \) is very close to 0 but positive, squaring \( x \) makes it even smaller (\( x^2 \) approaches 0). Thus, \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) becomes a very large positive number.

3. **Determine the Limit:** Since as \( x \to 0^+ \), \( x^2 \to 0^+ \), and consequently \( \frac{1}{x^2} \to +\infty \).

4. **Conclusion:** The limit of \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side is infinity.

The limit of the expression as \( x \) approaches 2 can be found by simplifying the function first. Factoring the numerator reveals common terms which cancel out, leading to a simplified linear function. Evaluating this at \( x = 2 \) gives the final result.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Original Expression:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}
   \]

2. **Factor Numerator and Denominator:**
   - The numerator \( x^2 - 4 \) is a difference of squares, which factors into \( (x + 2)(x - 2) \).
   - The denominator is already factored as \( (x - 2) \).

3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   \[
   \frac{(x + 2)(x - 2)}{x - 2} = x + 2
   \]
   *(Note: This simplification is valid for all \( x \neq 2 \), which includes near \( x = 2 \) when taking the limit.)*

4. **Evaluate the Limit of the Simplified Function:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 2} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

5. **Conclusion:**
   The limit is \( 4 \).

The limit of (x - sin(x))/x³ as x approaches 0 can be found using multiple methods:

**Taylor Series Expansion:**
- Expand sin(x) around 0: sin(x) = x - x³/6 + higher-order terms.
- Subtract from x: x - sin(x) ≈ x - (x - x³/6) = x³/6.
- Divide by x³: (x³/6)/x³ = 1/6.

**L'Hospital's Rule:**
- Apply L'Hospital's Rule thrice:
  1. f'(x) = 1 - cos(x), g'(x) = 3x² → (1 - cos(x))/(3x²).
  2. f''(x) = sin(x), g''(x) = 6x → sin(x)/(6x).
  3. f'''(x) = cos(x), g'''(x) = 6 → cos(x)/6.
- Evaluate at x=0: cos(0)/6 = 1/6.

**Trigonometric Identity:**
- Multiply numerator and denominator by (1 + cos(x)) to use sin²(x) identity:
  - Simplifies to [sin²(x)]/[3x²(1 + cos(x))] → [sin(x)/x]^2 * [1/(3(1 + cos(x)))].
- Evaluate limits: [1] * [1/6] = 1/6.

**Final Answer**

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

2. **Recall the Definition of \(e\):**
   - One of the definitions of Euler's number \(e\) is:
     \[
     e = \lim_{n \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{n}\right)^n
     \]

   - Here, as \(x \to \infty\), both the base and the exponent approach their respective limits in the definition of \(e\).

   - Therefore, substituting directly:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x = e
     \]

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 1 of the expression \(\frac{\sqrt[3]{x^3 + 1} - \sqrt[3]{2}}{x - 1}\), we can use rationalization and simplification techniques.

First, we recognize that this is a difference quotient for the function \( f(x) = \sqrt[3]{x^3 + 1} \). We need to compute the limit:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{\sqrt[3]{x^3 + 1} - \sqrt[3]{2}}{x - 1}
\]

We multiply the numerator and denominator by the conjugate factor of the cube root expression. Specifically, we use the identity for rationalizing cube roots:

\[
(\sqrt[3]{a} - \sqrt[3]{b})(\sqrt[3]{a^2} + \sqrt[3]{ab} + \sqrt[3]{b^2}) = a - b
\]

Let \( a = x^3 + 1 \) and \( b = 2 \). We multiply the numerator and denominator by \(\sqrt[3]{(x^3 + 1)^2} + \sqrt[3]{(x^3 + 1) \cdot 2} + \sqrt[3]{2^2}\):

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{\sqrt[3]{x^3 + 1} - \sqrt[3]{2}}{x - 1} \cdot \frac{\sqrt[3]{(x^3 + 1)^2} + \sqrt[3]{2(x^3 + 1)} + \sqrt[3]{4}}{\sqrt[3]{(x^3 + 1)^2} + \sqrt[3]{2(x^3 + 1)} + \sqrt[3]{4}}
\]

The numerator becomes:

\[
(x^3 + 1) - 2 = x^3 - 1
\]

The denominator remains as the product of \( (x - 1) \) and the conjugate factor. Thus, we have:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^3 - 1}{(x - 1)(\sqrt[3]{(x^3 + 1)^2} + \sqrt[3]{2(x^3 + 1)} + \sqrt[3]{4})}
\]

We factor the numerator \( x^3 - 1 \) as:

\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]

Canceling the common factor \( (x - 1) \) in the numerator and denominator, we get:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{\sqrt[3]{(x^3 + 1)^2} + \sqrt[3]{2(x^3 + 1)} + \sqrt[3]{4}}
\]

Substituting \( x = 1 \) into the simplified expression, we get:

- Numerator: \( 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3 \)
- Denominator: Each term becomes \( \sqrt[3]{(1 + 1)^2} = 2^{2/3} \), so the denominator is \( 3 \cdot 2^{2/3} \)

Thus, the limit is:

\[
\frac{3}{3 \cdot 2^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} = 2^{-2/3}
\]

The final answer is:

\[
\boxed{2^{-\frac{2}{3}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \), we can use the power rule for differentiation. Here's a step-by-step solution:

**Step 1: Rewrite the Function**

First, rewrite \( \frac{1}{x} \) using a negative exponent:
\[
\frac{1}{x} = x^{-1}
\]

**Step 2: Apply the Power Rule**

The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \). Applying this to our function:
\[
f(x) = x^{-1} \\
f'(x) = -1 \cdot x^{-1 - 1} \\
f'(x) = -x^{-2}
\]

**Step 3: Simplify the Expression**

To express the derivative in terms of a fraction, use positive exponents:
\[
-f(x) = -\frac{1}{x^{2}} 
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{1}{x^{\,2}}}
\]

To find the **second derivative** of the function \( f(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative
Using the power rule for differentiation, which states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = nx^{n-1} \):

\[
f(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x
\]

Differentiate term by term:

1. **First term**: \( \frac{d}{dx} x^4 = 4x^3 \)
2. **Second term**: \( \frac{d}{dx} (-3x^3) = -9x^2 \)
3. **Third term**: \( \frac{d}{dx} x = 1 \)

So, the first derivative \( f'(x) \) is:

\[
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative
Now, differentiate \( f'(x) \) to find the second derivative \( f''(x) \):

\[
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
\]

Differentiate term by term:

1. **First term**: \( \frac{d}{dx} 4x^3 = 12x^2 \)
2. **Second term**: \( \frac{d}{dx} (-9x^2) = -18x \)
3. **Third term**: \( \frac{d}{dx} 1 = 0 \)

So, the second derivative \( f''(x) \) is:

\[
f''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x

Using the quotient rule formula, the derivative of \( f(x) \) is:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{\cos{x} \cdot \cos{x} - \sin{x} \cdot (-\sin{x})}{\cos^2{x}} = \frac{\cos^2{x} + \sin^2{x}}{\cos^2{x}}
\]

Simplifying the numerator using the Pythagorean identity \( \sin^2{x} + \cos^2{x} = 1 \), we get:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2{x}} = \sec^2{x}
\]
</think>

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \tan{x} \), follow these steps:

   \[
   \tan{x} = \frac{\sin{x}}{\cos{x}}
   \]

2. **Apply the quotient rule** for differentiation, where if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u}{v} \), its derivative is given by:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
   \]

   - Let \( u = \sin{x} \)
   - Let \( v = \cos{x} \)

   - \( u' = \frac{d}{dx}(\sin{x}) = \cos{x} \)
   - \( v' = \frac{d}{dx}(\cos{x}) = -\sin{x} \)

6. **Simplify the numerator**:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\cos^2{x} + \sin^2{x}}{\cos^2{x}}
   \]

7. **Use the Pythagorean identity \( \sin^2{x} + \cos^2{x} = 1 \)** to simplify further:

To find the derivative of \( x^x \), I can start by taking the natural logarithm of both sides to simplify the differentiation process. Let’s let \( y = x^x \). Taking the natural log gives me \( \ln(y) = x \ln(x) \).

Next, I'll differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \). On the left side, using the chain rule, the derivative of \( \ln(y) \) is \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \). On the right side, applying the product rule to \( x \ln(x) \), I get \( 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).

Putting it all together, I have \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1 \). To solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \), I multiply both sides by \( y \), resulting in \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1) \).

Since \( y = x^x \), substituting back gives me the final derivative: \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

To find the derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \), we can use logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
2. **Take the natural logarithm of both sides:**  
   \( \ln(y) = \ln(x^x) \).
3. **Simplify using logarithm properties:**  
   \( \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x) \).
4. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**  
   - Left side: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \).  
   - Right side: Use the product rule on \( x \cdot \ln(x) \):
     \( \frac{d}{dx}(x \cdot \ln(x)) = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).
5. **Set the derivatives equal:**  
   \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1 \).
6. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**  
   Multiply both sides by \( y \):
   \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1) \).
7. **Substitute \( y = x^x \) back into the equation:**  
   \( \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\boxed{x^x (\ln(x) + 1)}

First, rewrite the function as \( (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \).

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use the **chain rule**. Here's a step-by-step solution:

### Step 1: Rewrite the Function
First, rewrite \( f(x) \) in a more convenient form for differentiation:
\[
f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
\]

### Step 2: Apply the Chain Rule
The chain rule states that if \( h(x) = g(u(x)) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:
\[
h'(x) = g'(u(x)) \cdot u'(x)
\]
In our case, let \( u(x) = x^2 + 1 \) and \( g(u) = u^{-1} \).

First, find the derivative of \( g(u) \) with respect to \( u \):
\[
g'(u) = -u^{-2}
\]

Next, find the derivative of \( u(x) \) with respect to \( x \):
\[
u'(x) = 2x
\]

### Step 3: Combine the Results Using the Chain Rule
Now, apply the chain rule:
\[
f'(x) = g'(u(x)) \cdot u'(x) = - (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x
\]
Simplify the expression:
\[
f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

### Final Answer
The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \) is:
\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{2 x}{\left(x^{2}+1\right)^{2}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we will use the **chain rule**. The chain rule is a fundamental technique in calculus for differentiating composite functions.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Let \( u = x^2 \) (inner function).
   - Then, the original function becomes \( e^{u} \) (outer function).

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to \( u \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} \left( e^{u} \right) = e^{u}
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} \left( x^2 \right) = 2x
   \]

4. **Apply the chain rule by multiplying the derivatives:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{x^2} \right) = \frac{d}{du} \left( e^{u} \right) \times \frac{d}{dx} \left( u \right) = e^{u} \times 2x
   \]

5. **Substitute \( u = x^2 \) back into the expression:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{x^2} \right) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
   \]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\frac{d}{dx}\left(e^{x^2}\right) = 2x e^{x^2}}
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Let's identify \( u(x) = \ln x \) and \( v(x) = x^2 \).

**Step 1:** Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):

  \]

  \[
  v'(x) = 2x
  \]

- Simplify by canceling one \( x \):
  \[

\[
h'(x) = g'(j(x)) \cdot j'(x)
\]

In this case:
- The outer function \( g(u) = \ln(u) \)

\]

\]

\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = x \ln x \), I will use the product rule. The product rule states that if you have a function which is the product of two functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative is \( u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \).

In this case, let me define:
\( u(x) = x \)
and
\( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, I'll find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
- The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( u'(x) = 1 \).
- The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

Now, applying the product rule:
\( f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \)
Substituting the derivatives I found:
\( f'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right) \)

Simplifying each term:
- The first term is \( \ln x \).
- The second term simplifies to \( 1 \) because the \( x \) in the numerator and denominator cancel out.

So, combining these terms gives me the final derivative of the function.

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = x \ln x \), we will use the product rule. 

**Product Rule:** If \( f(x) = u(x)v(x) \), then  
\( f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \)

Let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
- \( u'(x) = 1 \)
- \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \\
= (1)(\ln x) + (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \\
= \ln x + 1
\]

\[
\left( \frac{u}{v} \right)' = \frac{v u' - u v'}{v^2}
\]

where \(u = \sin x\) and \(v = x^2 + 1\). Calculating the derivatives:
- \(u' = \cos x\)
- \(v' = 2x\)

Substituting into the quotient rule formula:

\[
\frac{(x^2 + 1)\cos x - \sin x \cdot 2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

To find the second derivative of \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll follow these steps:

**First Derivative:**

We start by finding the first derivative using the chain rule.

\[
f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{x^2} \right) = e^{x^2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

**Second Derivative:**

Next, we find the second derivative by differentiating \( f'(x) \).

We use the product rule for differentiation:

\[
f''(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( 2x e^{x^2} \right)
\]

Applying the product rule (\( u'v + uv' \)), where \( u = 2x \) and \( v = e^{x^2} \):

\[
u' = 2, \quad v' = \frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

So,

\[
f''(x) = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot 2x e^{x^2} = 2e^{x^2} (1 + 2x^2)
\]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{f''(x)=2 e^{x^{2}} \left(1+2 x^{2}\right)}

2/(cos x cos(x+h)) * sin((2x + h)/2) * (sin(h/2)/(h/2)) * (1/2)

Because [sin(h/2)] / h = (sin(h/2))/(2*(h/2)) ) = 0.5 times [sin(h/2)/(h/2)]

So:

= 2/(cos x cos(x+h)) * sin((2x + h)/2) * 0.5 * [sin(h/2)/(h/2)] 

Which is:

(2*0.5)/[cos x cos(x+h)] times sin((2x + h)/2) times [sin(h/2)/(h/2)]

Simplifies to:

1/[cos x cos(x+h)] * sin((2x + h)/2) * [sin(h/2)/(h/2)]

As h approaches 0, (2x + h)/2 approaches x, so sin((2x + h)/2) approaches sin x. Similarly, sin(h/2)/(h/2) approaches 1.

Thus:

= [1/(cos x cos x)] * sin x * 1 = sin x / cos² x

Which matches our earlier result of sin(x)/cos²(x), or sec(x) tan(x). So the correct limit is indeed sin(x)/cos²x, and I made a mistake in factoring out constants earlier. The factor came out correctly when properly handling the terms.

Therefore, my initial confusion was because I messed up the constant factors during the limit process, but after correcting that, both methods agree on the derivative being sin x over cos squared x.

Just to make sure, let me consider plugging in a simple function where I know the derivative. For example, f(x) = tan(x). Its derivative is sec²x, which is 1 + tan²x or 1/cos²x. But wait, according to our result, d/dx (sec x) would be something else? Wait no.

Wait, actually, I'm getting mixed up with the derivatives of different functions. Let's clarify: f(x)=tan x has derivative sec² x because tan x = sin x / cos x. So let me compute that:

Using quotient rule on tan x = sin x / cos x:

Numerator derivative is cos x * cos x - (-sin x) * sin x over cos squared x.

Wait, actually, the derivative of numerator (sin x) is cos x times denominator minus numerator times derivative of denominator (cos x), which is -sin x. So it's [cos x * cos x - sin x*(-sin x)] / cos²x = (cos²x + sin²x)/cos²x = 1/cos²x, which matches.

So back to our problem: d/dx sec x = sin x / cos²x is correct. But since sec x is 1/cos x, another way to write its derivative would be:

Let me consider f(x) = (cos x)^(-1). Then f'(x) = (-1)(cos x)^(-2)*(-sin x) = sin x / cos²x. So that's consistent.

Thus, I've verified through multiple methods: quotient rule, product rule with the reciprocal identity, and first principles via trigonometric identities. All lead to the same conclusion that d/dx sec x is sin x over cos squared x.

Therefore, after thorough consideration and checking different approaches, I can confidently say that the derivative of secant is tangent times secant, which simplifies algebraically to sine divided by cosine squared.

**Final Answer**
The derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\) is \boxed{\sec x \tan x}\).
</think>

To find the derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\), we can use several methods including the quotient rule, product rule with reciprocal identities, and first principles.

1. **Using the Quotient Rule**:
   - Recall that \(\sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}\).
   - Let \(f(x) = ( \cos x )^{-1}\). Then, using the chain rule:
     \[
     f'(x) = (-1)(\cos x)^{-2}(-\sin x) = \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}
     \]
   - This simplifies to \(\sec x \tan x\) because \(\sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}\) and \(\tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x}\).

2. **Using the Product Rule**:
   - Express \(\sec x\) as \( \sec x = (\cos x)^{-1} \).
   - The derivative is then computed using the product rule with reciprocal identities, leading to the same result.

3. **First Principles (Definition of Derivative)**:
   - Using the limit definition of the derivative and trigonometric identities, we find that the limit evaluates to \(\frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}\).

4. **Verification**:
   - Cross-checking with known derivatives, such as the derivative of \(\tan x\) which is \(\sec^2 x\), confirms consistency.

Thus, after thorough consideration and checking different approaches, we conclude that the derivative of \(\sec x\) is \(\sec x \tan x\).

### Final Answer
The derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\) is \boxed{\sec x \tan x}\).
